### [Quick Thoughts on Overhang Arguments](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aJSdGMzgFRFBcBhGp/quick-thoughts-on-overhang-arguments)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have two goals for this post:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pose the question: Have </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/tag/computing-overhang" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">overhang</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> arguments been used successfully in other fields? </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lay out some of the reasons why I am suspicious of overhang arguments. </span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've had the second of these on my mind for a few years now and am posting something rough rather than leaving it in drafts. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intro</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Within AI safety, people sometimes use overhang arguments to justify doing things that appear suspicious. The core of an overhang argument, as I see it is: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are worried about some particular (dangerous) progress happening very quickly in the future.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can push forward at least one of the inputs to that progress today. We expect doing so will both bring the dangerous thing sooner and slow down future progress toward the dangerous thing. We're trying to trade time now for time later. </span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A common form of this is a "compute overhang" argument: let's avoid situations where there is tons of compute lying around and drastically more efficient algorithms get developed, as that would allow these algorithms to scale rapidly to ASI. Therefore, we should push forward algorithm efficiency as much as we can today (in addition to all of the non-compute inputs) while the compute stock is relatively low, and then progress in the future will be more limited by the rate of growth of compute stock (relatively slow and predictable). </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have overhang arguments been used successfully in other fields? </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have people made overhang arguments in other fields, have those arguments been compelling, what are the key similarities/differences to AI, and how has that gone? Based on asking LLMs, here are some examples (though all have key disanalogies):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled_burn" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Controlled/prescribed burns</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There are at least two disanalogies. First, controlled burns are not first-order very bad; if we were deliberately burning 1/10th of a village in order to reduce future risks to the village, that would be a different story. Second, controlled burns actually get rid of the stock of dry tinder, leading to smaller fires later. In a compute overhang argument, the more efficient algorithms are still available later. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Various cryptography examples including </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EFF_DES_cracker" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deep Crack</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In the late 1990s a bunch of cryptographers were worried that the commonly used DES encryption algorithm had too short of a key length. During a public competition, researchers developed Deep Crack which cracked a DES-encrypted message in 56 hours. This is considered to be one of the reasons why more secure encryption was adopted soon after. In effect, researchers worried that it would soon be possible to cheaply break DES; "Five years from now some teenager may well build a DES Cracker as her high school science fair project" (</span><a href="https://w2.eff.org/Privacy/Crypto/Crypto_misc/DESCracker/HTML/19980716_eff_descracker_pressrel.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com#:~:text=Five%20years%20from%20now%20some%20teenager%20may%20well%20build%20a%20DES%20Cracker%20as%20her%20high%20school%20science%20fair%20project.%22" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">source</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Therefore, they accelerated methods for this in order to show it was possible, still before it was easily accessible. A primary motivation was to show that a particular thing was dangerous and possible so that society addresses that risk, which appears different from many people's motivation for reducing compute overhang. I suspect that if the researchers knew for a fact that society would continue using DES regardless of their work, they wouldn't have tried to democratize its accessibility. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2010 </span><a href="https://science.house.gov/_cache/files/a/6/a6e0ddd6-f80d-4531-aae8-195dc5d9780d/B0E316E0DCB4666CE08B8BCD1061204E.020410-keith.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">David Keith made</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a combination of overhang arguments and other arguments for geoengineering funding. "it would be reckless to conduct the first large-scale SRM tests in an emergency. Instead, experiments should expand gradually to scales big enough to produce barely detectable climate effects and reveal unexpected problems, yet small enough to limit resultant risks". Keith was in favor of geoengineering and therefore likely viewed this as a question of how to develop a good thing responsibly. In the case of AI, it is pretty unclear whether we should be trying to (in the near term, given the state of alignment science) develop ASI at all, though some overhang proponents would disagree with me here. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Biological Gain of Function (GoF) Research. You run some risk of lab leaks in order to better understand and prepare for future biological threats. You might also publish information that makes it easier for others to develop a bioweapon. This is a preparedness argument more than an argument about slowing future progress. GoF research is much less diffused by default compared to AGI research—biology researchers aren't trying to sell their research to billions of customers and make billions of dollars in the process. EA-ish people seem to mostly oppose GoF research in the current environment, as does the U.S. government.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why I consider overhang arguments suspect</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In no specific order:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They are an "ends justify the means" consequentialist argument and a "the bad thing is good, actually" argument, which is in general suspect. Such arguments should require a high burden of proof.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They rely on complex reasoning about progress, and those making these arguments almost never spell out their full reasoning, let alone a detailed model of the situation (i.e., they don't meet the high burden of proof). I expect to have significant uncertainties and disagreements with whatever model is put forward, e.g., is the right way to think about algorithm progress as there being a fixed set of algorithms and we slowly pick higher and higher hanging fruit—is this true w.r.t. current margins? </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are two ways to reduce overhang: get rid of the overhanging resource (compute), or advance other resources (algorithms) earlier. The first looks better, yet those making overhang arguments rarely seem to advocate for this. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Empirically, the effect of trying to reduce compute overhang by advancing algorithms seems to have directly contributed to substantial compute buildout. One chain is Smarter AIs --&gt; demand from consumers --&gt; more compute buildout. Another is If you want to advance algorithms, you need lots of compute (so much so that compute for research </span><a href="https://epochai.substack.com/p/how-does-openai-allocate-its-compute" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might be the biggest category of compute spend</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) --&gt; compute buildout. Even though DeepSeek (and falling cost of intelligence) initially dropped U.S. stocks, I think the current consensus is that </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jevons_paradox" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jevons Paradox</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rules: cheaper intelligence causes increased demand for compute. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's very plausible to me that some of the people using overhang arguments are not primarily motivated by overhang arguments. It seems like they just want to do the dangerous-by-my-lights thing and this is an excuse. (e.g., </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/planning-for-agi-and-beyond/#fn-A" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Altman</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization#:~:text=I%20remember%20him%20arguing,build%20new%20chip%20fabs" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">discussion</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). These arguments seem super susceptible to motivated reasoning.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overhang arguments can be used to justify many seemingly-bad actions. One could argue that Covid was good because it reduced "society not being prepared for a pandemic" overhang. Seems sus. I am not sure where to draw the line between "clearly this is terrible" and "the overhang argument goes through".</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">List of relevant links</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are some links I've found useful when thinking about this topic</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a ### [The Alignment Problem: Computer Science Meets Analytic Philosophy](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><strong>TLDR:</strong> I explain what the technical AI alignment problem is and what its core difficulties are, in my view. This is a perspective that draws simultaneously on classical traditions in theoretical computer science / machine learning theory, ideas from analytic philosophy, and views that originated in the Machine Intelligence Research Institute / LessWrong.</p>
<h1>Minimal Viable Safety</h1>
<h2>The Task</h2>
<p>The problem of making AI safe is underspecified if we haven't decided what the AI is supposed to <em>do</em>. For example, we can run any AI inside a <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/2Wf3R4NZ77CLczLL2/cryptographic-boxes-for-unfriendly-ai">homomorphic encryption box</a> and never decrypt the output. This is probably perfectly safe<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span>, but also perfectly useless.</p>
<p>We can decide on an arbitrary task such as "construct Lean proofs for given mathematical theorems" and declare victory once we have safe AI that does it with superhuman skill<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>. However, that wouldn't address the danger. First, there would be incentives to use AI for tasks to which our solution doesn't apply. Second, even if we had precise safety guidelines for any task that anyone cares about, there would always be actors that ignore the guidelines, and we probably need Artificial Superintelligence (ASI) to reliably stop them.</p>
<p>If anyone, anywhere, creates an unrestricted unaligned ASI agent (more details about the word "agent" later), without an aligned ASI agent already in place to counteract it, then a catastrophic outcome is highly likely. I will not go into the reasons here: this has been discussed elsewhere at length, see for example the books I cited in the preface. Therefore, this event is something which we must never allow to happen. This is the main threat model that I set out to address.</p>
<p>Imagine a world in which anyone with sufficient resources has the ability to destroy the world. Like today, many will foolishly believe that instead of destroying the world, this act will bring them power and glory. Moreover, as computing hardware improves and new algorithms are developed, the amount of resources for this act will go down from year to year. Even assuming best-case regulation and international agreements, how long until someone does it? Can mere humans build a society which will be foolproof against such a risk over the course of generations? Resilient to any political upheavals that might come, and undoubtedly will? I find it vanishingly unlikely. Eventually we will need the help of ASI itself. Therefore, the <em>minimal</em> task that safe AI must be able to solve is "create a reliable global defense system against dangerous AI" (in the following, GDS).</p>
<h2>The Problem</h2>
<p>Consider the consequences of this task for the capabilities that the AI needs to have. It needs to reason about the entire planet<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span>. It needs to reason about all aspects of the problem, including scientific, technological, social, political and moral<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span>. It needs to have preferences about the state of the world. It needs to make long-term plans (guided by these preferences) that would play out decades, if not centuries, into the future. And it needs to do all that on a superhuman level.</p>
<p>In this light, here is how I (informally) define the "technical AI alignment problem". The problem is designing an AI system that can bring scalable (and ultimately superhuman) capability to bear on problems that require multi-domain reasoning and open-world long-term planning with far-reaching consequences, all the while staying aligned with the intentions and preferences of its human users.</p>
<h2>The Methodology</h2>
<p>What kind of methodology is appropriate for this problem? In the past, I <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/TALmStNf6479uTwzT/ai-alignment-metastrategy">argued</a> that it requires emphasis on deep theoretical understanding, rather than the incremental trial-and-error approach that is employed in the AI industry today. I won't rehash the entire debate here, but let me recap the core argument.</p>
<p>The nature of the incremental approach is as follows: we build a system, then we find problems<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>, then we make changes to fix these problems to the extent necessary for commercial viability in the current legal environment. We make some improvements; new problems arise (sometimes because of the improvements, sometimes because of new applications or other reasons); rinse and repeat. <em>This approach utterly fails if the "problems" result in AI takeover and possibly human extinction: there is nobody left to fix them.</em></p>
<p>In order for the incremental approach to succeed in preventing catastrophe, we need to reach a point where <em>no</em> new substantial problems arise, even inside the lab (before the system is deployed). Moreover, we need to reach this point <em>before</em> AI capabilities are sufficient for AI to escape our control. There are few reasons to expect such a miracle. On the contrary, sufficiently advanced AI might understand that it is in its interest to "lie low" (i.e. avoid visibly misaligned behaviors) until its capabilities cross the relevant threshold, such that the problems will only become apparent too late.</p>
<p>In contrast, the sane approach is creating the theoretical tools necessary to predict and prevent the problems before they are empirically observed. These tools need to remain applicable as AI capabilities rise substantially beyond human, for whatever algorithms, architectures and training protocols are used to achieve these capabilities. Moreover, these tools need to be extremely reliable for us to rationally trust them with the safety of our entire species.</p>
<h2>The Challenge</h2>
<p>While the theory of machine learning and AI is an established field with ample literature, its current state falls well short of the challenge. The core difficulties can be summarized as follows:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Hypercomplexity:</strong> It is infeasible to explicitly specify user preferences outside narrow domains, and narrow domains are not enough. Nor is it possible to explicitly specify a satisfactory model of the world. Therefore, learning is necessary.</li>
<li><strong>Alignment Grounding:</strong> When trying to learn user preferences, it is unclear what loss function or success metric can work outside narrow domains. Nor is it clear how to specify such a metric for <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/realism-about-metaphysics/">metaphysically realist</a> world-models, which are necessary here. Nor is it even clear what type of mathematical objects can formally describe these concepts.</li>
<li><strong>Alignment Convergence:</strong> Even given a success metric, it is unclear how to produce guarantees for optimizing this metric, given the ubiquity of irreversible mistakes and the lack of a theory of generalization for powerful learners.</li>
<li><strong>Side-Channel Resilience:</strong> The usual abstraction of "learner" is insufficient here, since we need to ensure the safety of internal computations rather than just external outputs.</li>
</ol>
<p>In the following, we will examine these difficulties one by one. But first, let's discuss what we need to accomplish.</p>
<h1>Alignment is Holistic</h1>
<p>Per the discussion above, our interest lies in AI systems that have open-world preferences and make effective plans to advance these preferences. In particular, making such plans requires learning a sophisticated model of the world and updating the model and the plan as additional information becomes available. We will call such a system an <em>AI agent</em>.</p>
<p>In order for an AI agent to be aligned, it needs to have aligned <strong>preferences</strong> and <strong>beliefs</strong>, and follow an aligned <strong>decision-making algorithm</strong> on the basis of these preferences and beliefs. While discussions about AI alignment often focus on the "preferences" part, beliefs and decisions are equally important. (Although it's true that preferences bring extra wrinkles into the problem as they are, arguably, the most "subjective" component in the trio.)</p>
<p>In this context "aligned" preferences typically means preferences very similar to those of the user(s)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span>, at least within some domain that we are somehow constrained to inhabit. On the other hand, "aligned" beliefs doesn't mean beliefs that are similar to those of the user: on the contrary, we expect the AI agent to acquire <em>more</em> accurate beliefs. They need to be beliefs that the user can rationally <em>trust</em> to be accurate. Similarly, "aligned" decision-making needs to be such that the user can rationally trust it to be in their own favor given aligned preferences and beliefs.</p>
<p>The philosophically astute reader will notice that at this point we are already in a world of trouble. What does it mean for the user to have <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/preferences/">preferences</a>? Are there objectively rational theories of <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/epistemology/">epistemology</a> and <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-theory/">decision-making</a>, and if so, what are these theories? Can beliefs or decisions be rational to hold or make "on behalf" of the user even if the user themself doesn't understand why? On the other hand, a computer scientist might be tempted to ignore these difficulties and just forge ahead with a solution that seems pragmatically satisfying. I think there is a lot to be said for such a practical mindset, and in this article I will aspire to embrace it. Unfortunately, we will see that the philosophical difficulties are not easily brushed aside.</p>
<p>Returning to the trichotomy of preferences, beliefs and decisions, observe that it is not even possible to entirely separate the three. For us to ascribe preferences to a system, it must rationally form beliefs and make decisions on the basis of these preferences. For us to ascribe beliefs to a system, it must rationally make decisions on the basis of these beliefs in the context of its preferences. And the rationality of decisions can only be judged in the context of specific preferences and beliefs.</p>
<p>Imagine an AI agent that pursues the happiness of mankind and is irrationally convinced that every human who dies goes to Heaven, which is the best possible place for human happiness. Such an AI would be motivated to kill all humans, even though its preferences are arguably "aligned". Moreover, since the belief in Heaven is unfalsifiable, such an AI can still be extremely capable at acting on its motivation and hence extremely dangerous.</p>
<p>If this thought experiment seems strange, consider the following: our beliefs would seem very strange to people who lived centuries ago. Therefore, it is highly likely that the beliefs of an ASI, capable of intellectual progress much faster than humans, will (and should) quickly become strange to us. The question is whether these strange beliefs will be produced by a process that we should deem trustworthy nevertheless.</p>
<p>"Alignment" is meaningless outside the full context of epistemic and instrumental rationality. Therefore, any theory of alignment has to be, in particular, a theory of epistemic and instrumental rationality.</p>
<h1>Hypercomplexity</h1>
<p>Perhaps the most simplistic approach to ensuring that an AI agent has aligned preferences and beliefs is explicitly specifying the preferences and beliefs that the agent should have. However, this is intractable. Human preferences are notoriously complex and giving a precise and complete mathematical specification of them seems hopeless. (Yudkowsky calls this "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/complexity-of-value">complexity of value</a>".) It seems no easier to accomplish than identifying images of cats by a handcrafted mathematical specification of "cat".</p>
<p>For weak agents, a practical solution can be explicitly specifying a <em>proxy</em> utility function that approximates our actual preferences within a particular domain. For example, you can imagine an agent designed to maximize customer reviews, ad clicks or stock value. Unfortunately, for sufficiently strong agents, this approach becomes prone to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/goodhart-s-curse">Goodhart's Curse</a>: the maximum of any such utility function is almost always at a point where it strongly diverges from our true preferences. For example, to maximize customer reviews one can leave many fake five-star reviews and prevent anyone from leaving bad reviews. In fact, it's preferable to ensure maximal control of the universe to prevent anything or anyone else from interfering with the stream of reviews.</p>
<p>One approach to avoiding Goodhart's Curse is somehow restricting the domain within which the agent operates or its overall impact (see e.g., <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.10720">Armstrong and Levinstein</a>). But this is incompatible with the GDS task.</p>
<p>Nor is it possible to directly specify the agent's beliefs. Even if we were satisfied with the agent having merely the sum total of humanity's current knowledge, formally specifying all of it would be a Herculean feat. <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.06624">Dalrymple et al.</a> proposed to attempt it (or something close) nevertheless, relying on assistance from weak AI systems. However, even if that were accomplished, it would be unsatisfactory. Indeed, a crucial ingredient in superintelligence, perhaps <em>the</em> crucial ingredient, is the ability to create sophisticated world models. Therefore, it is dubious that true superintelligence can be achieved while hobbling the system to only use knowledge we already have.</p>
<p>It makes even less sense to directly specify the agent's decisions: if we knew what decisions it should make, we wouldn't need the agent in the first place. Maybe it does make sense to have a closed-form algorithm that computes the decisions from preferences and beliefs. However, this requires strong assumptions on the form these beliefs take—for arbitrary models, the planning problem is often NP-hard or even PSPACE-hard, if not worse. I think that imposing such an assumption is actually reasonable and necessary (more on that in the next posts). But in any case, we cannot just delegate the task to a heuristic, since that would preclude any strong guarantee about the outcome<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<h1>Breakdown of Learning</h1>
<p><em>This section goes into more technical detail than the rest of the article, and can be skipped on a cursory reading.</em></p>
<p>Given that it's infeasible to directly specify aligned preferences and beliefs, it is a reasonable suggestion to <em>learn</em> them instead. To understand what would be involved, let's start by examining a general machine learning problem, where we need an algorithm with theoretical guarantees. We can decompose the problem into parts that I call <strong>grounding</strong> and <strong>convergence</strong>.</p>
<p>The grounding problem consists of the following:</p>
<ul>
<li>Define a mathematical object  which represents the unknown quantity we need to learn (for example, the parameters of an ideal classifier or of the environment MDP).</li>
<li>Determine what kind of data  we are going to use for learning (in particular, this involves some protocol about how the learner interacts with the environment and receives feedback).</li>
<li>Identify a mathematical relationship between ,  and the decisions of the learner, based on the physical process which produces them. Typically, given  and the learner policy  we should be able to describe a set  of possible data probability distributions<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span>.</li>
<li>Define a regret function  which measures how much worse the learner is doing compared to some target benchmark (we hide the details of the interaction between  and  inside this notation). Typically, the benchmark is something like the performance of an optimizer that knows  on the given task<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-9">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span>.</li>
</ul>
<p>For a grounded machine learning problem, it is roughly clear what the optimal learner would look like, if we ignore computational constraints. Formally, given a prior  over the parameter <span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-10">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span>, we can try producing the policy</p>
<p></p>
<p>However, it is not clear whether the learner's best effort would actually be good enough. For this, we need to solve the convergence problem, namely:</p>
<ul>
<li>Find a computationally feasible algorithm that produces  or a satisfactory replacement thereof.</li>
<li>Prove that in the limit of increasing amount  of data, learning actually succeeds. Formally, we can state it as</li>
</ul>
<p></p>
<ul>
<li>Prove a quantitative bound that shows this limit converges sufficiently fast in practice, even when exact minimization over  is replaced by the feasible algorithm.</li>
</ul>
<p>We will see that in the case of alignment, both grounding and convergence produce formidable challenges.</p>
<h1>Alignment Grounding</h1>
<p>What data can be used to learn the user's preferences? Many approaches have been proposed or attempted, for example:</p>
<ul>
<li>Human ratings of agent behaviors.</li>
<li>Human-rated real or fictional examples of behaviors or situations.</li>
<li>Human behavior (revealed preferences)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-11">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-11">[11]</a></sup>
    </span>.</li>
<li>Human influence on the environment (see <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.04198">Shah et al.</a>).</li>
</ul>
<p>The fundamental problem is that none of this data is a <em>direct and accurate</em> representation of human preferences. Humans make mistakes. Humans change their mind about what is preferable: sometimes because of new information, sometimes because of new experiences, sometimes just after thinking more about the question. Humans can prefer an outcome for terminal reasons (because it is valuable in itself) or for instrumental reasons (because it helps to bring about another outcome in the future), and none of this data even keeps track of the difference.</p>
<p>While it's clear that these sources of data have information about human preferences, accurately extracting this information requires understanding the precise relationship between human preferences and the process generating the data; whereas if this relationship is misspecified, then even perfect learning will produce misaligned preferences. And even if these misaligned preferences approximate the true preferences in some domain, Goodhart's Curse will push the agent to leave this domain and produce a catastrophic outcome.</p>
<p>For example, suppose that the agent is trained by human ratings of traces of agent actions and observations. An ideal learning process would then align the agent to the expected rating, conditional on the trace appearing in the training data. Such an agent is probably catastrophically misaligned for multiple reasons:</p>
<ul>
<li>Human raters might be wrong in their judgment, and their judgment will systematically become worse in worlds radically transformed by AI.</li>
<li>Human raters are subject to manipulation by the agent. A superintelligence might be able to exploit this to an extreme degree.</li>
<li>Some action-observation traces are unlikely to appear in the training data for "normal" reasons, for example traces that show the agent executing an AI takeover in a manner that is infeasible to fake<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-12">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-12">[12]</a></sup>
    </span>. Therefore, if such a trace did appear in the training data, it was rated by some abnormal process, for example by the rogue AI itself.</li>
</ul>
<p>We need a deep understanding of how human preferences are related to physical processes such that this relationship can be used as a source of ground truth. In order to be useful and precise, this understanding should be part of a mathematical theory. However, it is not even obvious what the <em>type</em> of "preferences" is. If we accept <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Neumann%E2%80%93Morgenstern_utility_theorem">VNM theory</a>, preferences should be described as a utility function. But what is the domain of this function? In computer science, it is popular to think of the domain as the action-observation trace of the agent. This, however, is deeply unsatisfying. First, such utility functions cannot be compared between different agents (each agent has its own trace), so "alignment" doesn't make any sense for them. Second, there is a strong case that humans have preferences about the physical reality itself, rather than merely their subjective experience of this physical reality.</p>
<p>What about beliefs? Is there an easy-to-specify ground truth for them? The answer is "yes" if the domain of beliefs is supposed to be traces, since the trace is directly observable by the agent. However, if preferences need a domain other than traces, then so do beliefs. Moreover, how can we make sense of belief alignment if the agent's beliefs are incomparable with the user's beliefs? The traditional computer science approach here is akin to <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/logical-empiricism/">logical positivism</a> in analytic philosophy, which we should abandon in favor of <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/realism-about-metaphysics/">metaphysical realism</a> (more on that in the next posts).</p>
<h1>Alignment Convergence</h1>
<p>Even if we knew how to ground the alignment learning problem, we'd face another problem: learning fails (i.e. doesn't converge at all) when irreversible mistakes are possible. Indeed, learning requires exploration<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-13">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-13">[13]</a></sup>
    </span>, but in the real world unlimited exploration produces irrecoverable mistakes. For example, if a robot explores what happens when it jumps out of a 10th-floor window, it won't have the chance to learn from this mistake. On the other hand, there is, strictly speaking, no way to be certain that jumping out of a window is a <em>bad</em> idea if you never try it. This is why regret bounds in <a href="https://rltheorybook.github.io/rltheorybook_ABJKS.pdf">reinforcement learning theory</a> always require an assumption such as episodicity (the environment resets after every "episode") or communication (any state of the environment's Markov Decision Process is reachable from any other state), which rules out irreversible mistakes by fiat.</p>
<p>This observation is not merely a technical nitpick. On the contrary, the existence of irreversible mistakes is a core feature of the world that makes the alignment problem difficult. Indeed, the alignment problem itself is our attempt to avoid the irreversible mistake of creating unaligned superintelligence. Any AI agent faces its own version of the problem when considering the creation of a superior successor agent. A GDS agent can commit an irreversible mistake by allowing a rival superintelligence to emerge. And of course, there are more prosaic examples, such as killing a person or failing to prevent their death<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-14">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-14">[14]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>The typical theoretical approach to navigating irreversible mistakes is <em>Bayesian planning</em>. Namely, we postulate a <em>prior</em> over the space of possible environments and then choose a policy with maximal expected long-term utility with respect to this prior. In theory, this produces an optimal balance between exploration, exploitation and caution. However, this leads to the difficult question of what prior to use<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-15">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-15">[15]</a></sup>
    </span>. Another question is how to implement Bayesian planning, given that it's computationally intractable in any remotely realistic setting<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-16">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-16">[16]</a></sup>
    </span>. Arguably, we should reconcile Bayesian planning with the machine learning paradigm by approximating Bayesian planning via meta-learning, but the theoretical foundation for such a method is an intricate open problem (see the next posts for more details).</p>
<p>If so, the hope is that we can reduce planning to learning, not via conventional reinforcement learning, but via a more sophisticated meta-learning paradigm. But, granting this reduction, we need to face yet another problem. Namely, we have no useful theory of generalization for highly expressive learning algorithms (such as deep learning), i.e. algorithms that can efficiently learn sophisticated compositional models.</p>
<p>One may ask: isn't it enough that deep learning works in practice? Why do we need a theory of generalization? Three reasons:</p>
<ul>
<li>We need to know that, under verifiable assumptions, our grounding method will provide the agent with sufficient knowledge about the relevant user attributes, in time for the agent to make the right decisions.</li>
<li>We can't just add data and compute and tweak the algorithm until it works, because an intermediate point in this process might produce catastrophic results. For example, some humans are smart enough to build ASI but not smart enough to either make sure it's aligned or not to build it. As a user, I would not like my agent to inhabit a similar epistemic state, even if the agent <em>would</em> be fully aligned with more scaling.</li>
<li>Solving the grounding problem requires a theory of intelligent agency that applies to humans, and since learning is a core ingredient of intelligence, the theory of agency requires a theory of generalization as one of its pillars.</li>
</ul>
<h1>Side-Channel Resilience</h1>
<p>It is standard in computer science to focus on the input-output behavior of an algorithm and its resource cost but ignore other side-effects of its execution. We care that we get the right answer and that it doesn't cost too much time, space, etc., but we usually don't care how it heats the hardware or what kind of electromagnetic effects it creates. Typically, we also don't worry about whether it happens to trigger a bug in the operating system or other software scaffolding around it. Indeed, such considerations are not even possible without knowing the full software and hardware context in which the algorithm is implemented. Moreover, computing hardware, operating systems, etc. are intentionally designed to support this abstraction.</p>
<p>It is well known that this way of thinking can fail when it comes to cybersecurity (see e.g., <a href="https://eprint.iacr.org/2005/388">Zhou and Feng 2005</a>, <a href="https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-0-387-71829-3_2">Standaert 2009</a>, <a href="https://www.mdpi.com/2410-387X/4/2/15">Randolph and Diehl 2020</a>). There are many clever ways in which attackers can exploit bugs and side-effects to create outcomes which are not possible in the abstract, "denotational" model of the system. In the context of the alignment problem, it is not external malign actors that we worry about<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-17">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-17">[17]</a></sup>
    </span> but attacks coming from within the agent itself.</p>
<p>In the context of the theory of agents, classical abstractions lead to <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/behaviorism/"><em>behaviorist</em></a> interpretations. Any guarantee about the preferences, beliefs and decisions of the agent would reference only the actions that the agent takes through its intended interface. However, such guarantees might not reflect the actual relevant attributes of the agent. We can imagine a superintelligent agent that intentionally produces only benign behavior across its intended interface, while simultaneously conducting a malign side-channel attack<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-18">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-18">[18]</a></sup>
    </span>. Such an agent can satisfy behaviorist formal safety guarantees and remain catastrophically dangerous in practice. For example, think of a robot that never takes a malign action with its actuators, but runs an internal computation which causes an unaligned successor agent to be copied into a different system.</p>
<p>This is not to say that we are to abandon abstraction. Indeed, it is implausible that the solution to the alignment problem must or should depend on, e.g., the details of the hardware or the operating system. Instead, we need to develop new abstractions which would be better suited for the problem at hand, abstractions that go beyond pure behaviorism.</p>
<h1>Inner and Outer Alignment</h1>
<p>So far I have described my preferred perspective on the alignment problem. A different frame, popular in the AI alignment community, is "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/inner-alignment">inner</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/outer-alignment">outer</a> alignment". IMO the inner/outer alignment frame is less precise, but can still be a useful source of intuition. It is hence worthwhile to understand the connection between these views, and in particular to see how my perspective captures the "mesa-optimizer" threat model.</p>
<p>First, let me briefly recap what a mesa-optimizer is (following <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.01820">Hubinger et al. 2019</a>). For the purpose of this argument, we are thinking of the agent as an episodic reinforcement learning algorithm. Then, a mesa-optimizer is a learned policy which is <em>an agent in itself</em>. Crucially, the mesa-optimizer might have a reward function different from that of the outer-loop reinforcement learner. There are two reasons why this might happen:</p>
<ul>
<li>The inner (mesa-optimizer) reward might have been close to the outer reward on previous episodes, which "fooled" the outer-loop learning algorithm. This is consistent with the inner reward drastically departing from the outer reward in yet-unexplored regions of state-space.</li>
<li>The mesa-optimizer might be planning on a time horizon much longer than a single episode. In this case, it has the incentive to intentionally hide its misaligned preferences in order to be stabilized by the outer loop.</li>
</ul>
<p>If the outer-loop learning algorithm is allowed to continue, it will either eventually remove the mesa-optimizer or force the mesa-optimizer to forever display behavior aligned with the outer reward. However, if the environment allows irreversible processes (as the real world does), then the mesa-optimizer can bide its time until an opportune moment to irreversibly push the system into a region of state-space favored by the inner reward (and typically unfavored by the outer reward). This is closely related to the "sharp left turn" of <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/GNhMPAWcfBCASy8e6/a-central-ai-alignment-problem-capabilities-generalization">Soares 2022</a>.</p>
<p>Under this model, "outer alignment" means that the outer reward function is close to that of the user, whereas "inner alignment" means that the inner reward function is (globally) close to the outer reward function. Each of them is a non-trivial problem.</p>
<p>A possible real-world example of mesa-optimization is the evolution of <em>Homo sapiens</em>. Arguably, we can model evolution as a reinforcement learning algorithm whose reward function is reproductive fitness. Evolution created humans who are agents (and in particular reinforcement learners), but the human reward function is different—if all we cared about were reproductive fitness, we would never have invented birth control (or at least, it would be much less popular). Given enough time, natural selection should be able to "fix" this, but it's probably never going to happen because human society and technology evolve much faster towards other ends, which is irreversible from evolution's vantage point.</p>
<p>Would addressing the problems in the previous sections address inner alignment as well? I argue that it would. If we had a guarantee that the agent's attributes are aligned, then, in particular, it wouldn't be controlled by a misaligned mesa-optimizer. These guarantees are supposed to take irreversible processes into account, so the "sharp left turn" is ruled out. These guarantees are supposed to include both direct effects and side-effects, ruling out both attack vectors for mesa-optimizers (in the terminology of the original LTA <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/5bd75cc58225bf0670375575/the-learning-theoretic-ai-alignment-research-agenda#Taming_daemons">article</a>, direct effects correspond to "Cartesian daemons" and side-effects to "non-Cartesian daemons").</p>
<p>More constructively, the outer vs. inner alignment dichotomy approximately corresponds to alignment of preferences vs. alignment of beliefs and decisions. Indeed, if we think of the outer loop as model-based reinforcement learning, then the learned policy comes from a learned model, in which case a misaligned inner agent corresponds to a misaligned <em>belief</em>. (For example, Christiano's <a href="https://ordinaryideas.wordpress.com/2016/11/30/what-does-the-universal-prior-actually-look-like/">argument</a> that "the universal prior is malign" can be viewed as describing such a misaligned belief.) Alternatively, if we think of the outer loop as model-free reinforcement learning, then the misaligned inner agent corresponds to misaligned <em>decision-making</em> (although I think that the model-based view is more useful).</p>
<h1>Conclusion</h1>
<p>We have seen that solving the alignment problem requires a body of theory that can tackle the core challenges of the problem: alignment grounding, alignment convergence and side-channel resilience, in the context of holistic alignment and hypercomplexity. This taxonomy is not necessarily intended as a decomposition where each subproblem is possible to solve separately—while thinking of them as subproblems can be fruitful, ultimately they are interconnected. (For example, alignment grounding requires modeling the user as an agent, which requires understanding how agents deal with irreversible mistakes, which is a subproblem of alignment convergence.) It <em>is</em> intended as a set of lenses that must be applied to any proposed solution in order to validate its viability.</p>
<p>In the following posts we will discuss the emerging computational agent theory, and see how it is building the tools to address all of these difficulties.</p>
<p><em>I thank Daniel Carmon, Fernando Garcia, Jeremy Gillen, Matthias Georg Mayer, Vinayak Pathak and my beloved spouse <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/marcus-ogren?mention=user">@Marcus Ogren</a> for diligently reviewing drafts. Their feedback greatly improved this article. All remaining flaws are entirely my own fault.</em></p>

</li>

</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-12"><p>Can we fake any hypothetical scenario in the training data? No, because the real world is complex beyond any synthetic scenario, and since , a bounded agent can tell the difference. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-12">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-13"><p>Except in frameworks like full-feedback, which are unrealistic in our context. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-13">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-14"><p>Strictly speaking, virtually everything in the real world is irreversible—this is essentially the second law of thermodynamics. </li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-15"><p>We can imagine that there is some "objective" prior, or that the prior is a subjective property of the user that needs to be learned. See some initial exploration of this problem <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/dPmmuaz9szk26BkmD/vanessa-kosoy-s-shortform?commentId=H8xmLq9WRz9j8KjJa">here</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-15">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-16"><p>For example, it's <a href="https://cstheory.stackexchange.com/questions/41396/is-bayes-optimal-rl-of-a-finite-set-of-dfas-feasible">NP-hard</a> even for a small mixture of small deterministic state machines. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-16">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-17"><p>Of course, in a real-world project, we should <em>also</em> worry about the external malign actors. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-17">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-18"><p>Besides side-channel attacks in the usual sense, another concern is "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/mindcrime">mindcrime</a>": a scenario in which the agent performs computations which are abhorrent in themselves, such as detailed simulations of suffering humans. This concern is valid IMO, but not necessary to motivate side-channel resilience. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-AsXrDEKLZnorctKEP-18">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### ["Holy shit" graphs and how to get more of them](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GK9p6NHEk8bkZQxH4/holy-shit-graphs-and-how-to-get-more-of-them-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are likely in a situation where the most powerful models in the world are internal models, and, here's a key distinction, potentially even models that are </span><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/08/14/anthropic-model-2-ai-risk" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">never intended to be released to the public</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (i.e. a model designed to provide internal uplift on capabilities during training but that is not intended to be / not particularly suitable for commercial purposes.) </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal-external information and capability gaps can distort both public and regulatory perception of frontier capabilities and dangers. If the internal frontier is three or six months ahead,  such a gap is worrisome if the labs are close to automating various bottlenecks to increased capabilities development, like automating AI R&amp;D.</span><br /><br /><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some ways of mitigating these visibility gaps and communicating the existing data we do have are implementable over the short term and confer minimal cost for many actors relative to the advantages of visibility for policymakers.</span></b><br /><br /></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is a "holy shit" graph?</span></b><br /></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of my intuitions about the coming impacts of AI are shaped by "Holy shit" graphs. The METR time horizon graph is one such graph, but has been discussed to death</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyuyrjhzb2m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyuyrjhzb2m">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so let's talk about another three:</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal spending by OpenAI researchers (at API prices)</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791982010/lexical_client_uploads/jlm60eqrf0kgqizt3qtb.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: Epoch AI, "Coding-agent spending by OpenAI researchers is doubling roughly every month", adapted from OpenAI data, Oct. 2026</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This spending graph is so bananas that when I try to google the source, The Gemini overview sometimes gets "AI token spending" and "Internal AI token spending by OpenAI researchers" confused and asserts that the thing I'm asking about could not possibly be true.</span><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791981046/lexical_client_uploads/h2v6mb0ezzaksm6td1sg.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yeah man, no kidding.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epoch concludes:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Such rapid growth is probably unsustainable: if the recent growth in our preferred models continued for another year, annualized run rates would reach about $185 million for the median researcher and $2.3 billion for the 90th percentile researcher.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the fuck.</span><br /></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">General AI (as opposed to narrow AI) model revenues</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792025432/lexical_client_uploads/o6ymaqmashjv3fixaetp.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: Azeem Azhar et al @ Exponential View, "The state of the AI economy" , June 2026</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This graph</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefovz1k9asl9s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnovz1k9asl9s">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> shows the growth in frontier AI company revenues over time. It's unclear from the data labeling but this number is growing at 3-4x per year for the industry. To get a sense for the growth estimates, we can look at a prettier but more out of date graph from Epoch instead.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792050313/lexical_client_uploads/qpjfravcm4pujt79sa0f.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: Epoch AI, "The Epoch AI Brief - September 2025"</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There seem to be some industry leader effects; Anthropic was growing faster when it was the second-highest company in revenue than now, and do note that this number </span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-217943723" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">may currently be slowing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and is itself an endogenous variable downstream of policy. Nevertheless, the industry as a whole is growing at a rate that would make it larger than the 2026 world economy by 2031-33. The point is not that this will continue forever, it's that if it doesn't stop continuing soon, the world must start looking very strange by the early 2030s, if not earlier.</span><br /></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extent of AI R&amp;D automation</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792027153/lexical_client_uploads/vsiiwe9vmkaptjy9zzkx.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: Anthropic, "Measurements for understanding the pace of AI development inside frontier labs", Aug/Sept. 2026</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's hard to see from this graph, but it seems like it is currently taking about 15 months from a category to go from low single digits to fully saturated, and about seven months for the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> category to reach roughly the same level that the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> graph was at. This would  seem to predict, again absent regulation, ~8 months from the end of the graph (6 months from now) to AL4-5 hitting ~99% and ~22 months from the end of the graph (20 months from now) for the AL5 category to hit 100%. Indeed, </span><a href="https://jack-clark.net/2026/05/04/import-ai-455-automating-ai-research/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic co-founder Jack Clark estimated back in the halcyon days of May 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a 60% chance that "an AI system powerful enough that it could plausibly autonomously build its own successor – happens by the end of 2028." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can and likely should discount this from its source, but discounting it and the many related arguments all the way to "nope, not going to happen" seems </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hopeful</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think communicating these "Holy shit" graphs, insofar as they are accurate, and we should have ways of verifying their accuracy externally, might be one of the most impactful pieces of communication the labs can give policymakers and the public. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, the accuracy of the first graph is weird. If we look back at the first few months of the graph, it would seem to imply that even though Codex launched in early 2025, it took until well into 2026 for Codex use to become widespread at the company. This is reasonable-ish speed of diffusion? But certainly non-obvious. </span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In any case, I'm not going to justify these claims here,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuha2n9mnfz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuha2n9mnfz">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but Anthropic</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2zequjia2ib"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2zequjia2ib">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reaching 100% AL5, and indeed even "only" substantial fractions of model R&amp;D work being at AL5, seems like a four to five-alarm fire loss of control risk. This graph is a good starting point in evaluating the case that we are a couple of years away from full AI R&amp;D on the default path, instead of 10 or more.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So it seems robust under many worldviews that independent evaluators and regulators being able to audit this information more comprehensively would be good thing. The graphs ought to be trustable, and indeed we should be cautious of those that are impossible to audit.</span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what makes a "Holy shit" graph, in this definition? Three things:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0th: Your graph encodes a model of reality that is reasonably accurate, to your best understanding. Your graph being honest is not a pre-requisite for it going viral, but I will be very sad if you take this post as encouragement to make irresponsibly misleading graphs :(</span></b><br /></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sustained trend over a non-trivial number of data points, and the trend doesn't yet seem to be budging.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extrapolating out the trend naively gives crazy results.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So there must be a trend break, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">surely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but the impacts of the trend break are likely to be severe as well.</span></li></ol><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the labs are sitting on a bunch of these data sources, and it would be good if we had more of them. Here's a few suggestions:</span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Increased training metric transparency:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Regularly updated</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeowire2ze5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneowire2ze5">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> series showing number of current training runs above some FLOP threshold (potentially </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/factpages/general-purpose-ai-obligations-under-ai-act" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in concordance with EU AI Act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), FLOP count of largest active training run, FLOP count of largest ever training run.</span><br /><br /><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Increased deployment metric transparency:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Time since end of training, total revenue breakdown for each model</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmt9di0mzrt7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmt9di0mzrt7">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Agent metrics:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Current count, 99.9th percentile length session (actual max might fail to preserve privacy, but might still be an important indicator of a rogue agent)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbvwwjdgsut"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbvwwjdgsut">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, number of websites </span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The high-level point is having a set of measurements which we can reference and which serve as high-level primary-source indicators of the 0th, 1st and 2nd derivatives of capabilities growth.</span></b><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But insight into speed of diffusion also matters, I would argue for both understanding the mechanics of diffusion and for study of gradual disempowerment. Much has been written about gradual disempowerment </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2m0htjthxrs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2m0htjthxrs">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and indeed I think it's a policy area that is as important or more to understand as even the x-risk landscape. But much less has been written about how to operationalize it, though the GD paper authors do cite this as important follow-up work. Part of the reason that less work has gone into these metrics is that existing labor automation tools capture it meaningfully, but there are facets of the problem that go beyond economic and labor impacts. I'd love to see work done to measure every all three sections of the paper. Here are some thoughts:</span></p><p><br /></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labor, relational and political</span></b></h3><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Amount of time spent on claude.ai or chatgpt.com, averaged across every American</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1argcfzopm4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1argcfzopm4">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or across the whole globe.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Amount of time spent on claude.ai or chatgpt.com, averaged across every American.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Measurements of aggregate token output, and some translation of said token output to a "human-equivalent hours worked" concept.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- Percentage of Americans that have most of their political content or discussion come from a chat application or agent.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This could be you!</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are you an individual researcher, research organization or group working on alignment? Maybe you could make some "Holy shit" graphs with datasets that are already public or could be made public if requested?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're a grantmaker or manager, area worth thinking about?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think METR and Epoch are, in important ways, highly cited in part </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they make "Holy shit" graphs. It also seems important to me that these graphs have distinctive visual style and branding, and that they are either hand-crafted by humans or that their AI contributions are not highly visible; if the same graph was in some sense "equally good" but designed by the visual style of even Opus 5.5</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoleausp828"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoleausp828">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, many would disengage from the graph.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This does not mean that you should pivot away from the wonderful work that you are currently doing and go make "holy shit" graphs. I do think the number of useful "Holy shit" graphs is relatively small, maybe 20 or 50? And many of the best ones require a lab to publish the relevant data product. But the more general point I'm trying to gesture at is that investing in communicating your work legibly to your audience of choice is highly cost effective.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhfc7k7m6h86"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhfc7k7m6h86">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe someone on your team </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">should </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be making "Holy shit" graphs, or identifying future "Holy shit" graphs you could make, or in general investing in communicating your research memorably, legibly, and in a way that gets your organization's logo around the internet a few hundreds of thousands of times.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that though I think this work is on average net positive, I am arguing it is also a force multiplier for your work. If many people currently think your work is net negative, consider engaging effortfully with those arguments before making the cool graph.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyuyrjhzb2m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyuyrjhzb2m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The METR time horizons graph has come under intense scrutiny recently. </span><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/against-the-metr-graph-coding-capabilities-software-jobs-task-ai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This piece</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> lays out a few reasons for why to distrust the graph. From most to least severe:</span><br /></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The experimental design paid the benchmarked study participants per hour, which can incentivize working longer than you would if you were paid per completion of task.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The sample size for the experiments was vanishingly small, with n=4 for some data points.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The METR task suite systematically overestimated task length, as the participants took a surprisingly long time getting oriented on task basics.</span><br /></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first point is a fairly strong criticism, and the second one should make us worry about the measurement error of the task suite, but the third point I would argue has the opposite sign. If we add a fixed time to each task or more realistically a time penalty that is decreasing in task length share, shorter tasks would get overestimated more than longer tasks are underestimated. This should make your estimate of the level biased upward and your estimate of the slope on a log plot biased downward. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But in any case, if we're worried about the external validity of the experiment, METR has designed other graphs with comparable results. Overall I agree that METR's time horizons graph is not literally, actually measuring "the thing we care about", but it's a close-enough correlate to the thing we care about that it's worth paying attention to. Imagine if we'd had a (non-saturated) mathematics research time horizon graph. I think it would have had enough internal validity to give slight advance warning of OpenAI's recent results.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similar, and imo equally non-fatal methodological critiques apply to the other graphs discussed here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnovz1k9asl9s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefovz1k9asl9s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a more updated graph, but I think that one's paywalled :( I think it would be very socially positive if this specific graph were not paywalled because the deduplication effects are neat and useful and address a central skepticism of frontier AI revenues. But even if this graph remains paywalled, the version made from lab internals need not be.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuha2n9mnfz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuha2n9mnfz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did a more detailed job of that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jbttuCF4wFZmXakcj/the-world-s-best-gradual-disempowerment-model-organism" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Other people have done much more detailed jobs elsewhere.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2zequjia2ib"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2zequjia2ib">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And any other lab, to be clear.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneowire2ze5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeowire2ze5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1x a week, targeting &lt;1 month reporting lag.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmt9di0mzrt7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmt9di0mzrt7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm particularly fond of these because they dovetail nicely with the </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q25-2026-timelines-update-uplift" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIFP's additional models for estimating the date of "automated coder."</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbvwwjdgsut"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbvwwjdgsut">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know that individual instances have hard timeouts, but my understanding is, especially with API pricing, it is possible to run a scheduler on an agent setup that more or less allows sequences of instances to act like one single autonomous agent.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2m0htjthxrs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2m0htjthxrs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not enough! GD is not just an outcome that we get if we avoid losing control quickly, it's also a potential precursor to losing control quickly, or to locking in trajectories where we lose control quickly.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1argcfzopm4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1argcfzopm4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I say Americans here, I'm saying so because the frontier labs have a disproportionate number of Americans in their customer base and because frontier labs are meaningfully based in the US, but this data would also be great to have for other regions, of course.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoleausp828"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoleausp828">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not to mention, I think the models are not quite at human expert level on visual design.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhfc7k7m6h86"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhfc7k7m6h86">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also have the impression that many alignment organizations are currently smaller than would be cost-effective, and am unsure that cost-effectiveness is even the right north star to be aiming at in this situation, but as with anything happy to discuss these things / change my mind.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GK9p6NHEk8bkZQxH4/holy-shit-graphs-and-how-to-get-more-of-them-1#comments">Discuss</a>

## C++

### [The Problem with C++ Safety (Part 1): Why “It Works” Isn’t Good Enough](https://www.modernescpp.com/index.php/cpp-and-memory-safety-part-1/)

**来源**: Modernes C++ (Rainer Grimm)

**摘要**: Rainer Grimm’s blog continues. In this first article of a new series, Klaus Iglberger looks at why C++ is called memory unsafe and argues that the real problem isn’t the language itself, but how we write and teach it.

## Engineering

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>
